## AI Research

### [Understanding why 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 + ... = -1/12 (rather than ∞)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/F7DNBdoADqktjn2Hk/understanding-why-1-2-3-4-5-1-12-rather-than-infinity)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic Status: I consider myself fairly well-versed in mathematics, with a decent-to-good understanding of Complex Analysis, etc. but I am </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a practicing mathematician.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're like me, then you're a bit saturated with explainer videos and posts purporting to explain this infamous, memetic math assertion:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> like me, you're annoyed that the popular ones don't really explain the most direct derivation, and often resort to shuffling sequences around to show how it kind of works if you squint.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp50q8mpp8l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp50q8mpp8l">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Not only is that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> how it is typically first explained to a curious math student, these explanations also often leave out important details about why it's interesting and why we should care.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though, as it turns out, I learned a decent bit myself making sure I got as much right in this post as I could.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before we say anything further: that equals sign is of course a bit of a lie. What </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> actually "equals", if it equals anything, is ∞, as one would expect. The equals sign here is really more of a "can be thought of as equivalent to, in some specific sense". And yet, it is somehow more than a mere mathematical parlor trick—if you insist on a physical-world "application"</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0efalm4i6les"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0efalm4i6les">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> there surprisingly is one in (spoilers) Section 3.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For this post, I'm expecting the reader to have a good grasp of Calc 1/2 and the ability to recognize things from Calc 3 if pointed out. A </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lot</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of what is written here is going to be oversimplification, or ignoring details. That simply comes with the territory, unfortunately, but I hope this post is less misleading than most.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, it wouldn't hurt to have a pop-sci level of understanding of Quantum Field Theory.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think of there being three approaches to explaining this strange formula:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The "Classic" approach</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, in Section 1, which was developed quite early and can be explained in one sentence to a student versed in Complex Analysis</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcpd7zx1vyga"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncpd7zx1vyga">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but is a little mysterious, and doesn't explain why the formula is important (and involves complex numbers).</span></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The "Smooth Sum" approach</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (which I prefer to call "Smooth Cutoff"), which does make things much clearer. Its cleanest exposition dates back to 2010, but some of the basic ideas stretch backward to Ramanujan in 1913.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2dmhf1u57he"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2dmhf1u57he">[4]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcl8oqzycmxt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncl8oqzycmxt">[5]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The "Manipulate sums until it just works" approach</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is closely related to 2, but tends to come off as trickery that explains little. I don't really cover this one.</span></li></ol><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Warning: this post contains math! While the goal here is to help readers build a better intuitive feel for the mathematics involved, it is not strictly necessary that you understand every step along the way. Simply getting the gist of what's going on is enough to help you better comprehend at the end how a fake equation like 1+2+3... = -1/12 can reflect how reality works.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Index:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Canonical Approach: Analytic Continuation and the Riemann Zeta Function</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Smooth Sums" of Sequences and Why -1/12 is Special</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quantum Field Theory, Regularization, and the Vacuum Energy</span></li></ol><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1 - The Canonical Approach: Analytic Continuation and the Riemann Zeta Function</span></b></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are Analytic Continuations?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is no writing this without slaying the dragon that many other explainers (to me, bizarrely) refuse to touch: Analytic Continuation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a way, Analytic Continuation is an answer to one of the most natural of starting math questions: given a set of points, can we connect them with a nice curve?</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The factorial function is defined only at the positive integers. Figure generated by Claude with matplotlib.</span></p></blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: Many plots here will be "I asked Claude to make this to illustrate a point", or else copied from Wikipedia or some other open-source image source. The interactive widgets were built by Claude and reviewed by Gemini Neon, which also wrote several of their captions.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The classic example is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the factorial function. Sure, it's technically undefined anywhere outside the nonnegative integers {0, 1, 2, ...}, but it </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sure looks like</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> there's some kind of curve you can draw connecting all the points.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But how would you define such a thing rigorously? You can take out a marker and draw all kinds of curves that technically hit all the points: curves that squiggle up and down, curves that are jaggedy, curves that asymptote to infinity every half-digit. What do we </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">want</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, roughly speaking, we want it to be a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">curve</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, something smooth that feels like it could be a natural function, like </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. To a mathematician, wanting something to be smooth suggests a function that is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">analytic</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">;</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4f1fweunwxq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4f1fweunwxq">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that is, one that is differentiable, has a second derivative, a third derivative, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ad infinitum</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefys6xg4pubgi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnys6xg4pubgi">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Essentially every natural function you could write down has this property (within its domain), and it's part of why they look, well, so </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">smooth</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So an analytic continuation (in the broadest sense) is: find a nice analytic curve that extends (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">continues</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) a function that has a restricted domain to other places, ideally the rest of the real number line (every possible real number from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) or, even better, the entire complex number plane (all possible complex numbers </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). There are techniques for generating such things, or, if you're clever enough, you can just make up an analytic function that fits the points and does what you want.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And in the case of the factorial function, we have made up the Gamma function:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(OK, technically </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, rather than </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The reasons for this are historical: </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrien-Marie_Legendre" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Legendre</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4ulaxhkmsdf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4ulaxhkmsdf">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, who defined the modern gamma function, chose to do it this way. It makes some formulas nicer and other formulas messier. It has stuck.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm not going to go through the exercise of proving that the Gamma function equals the factorial at all positive integers</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqhjjm287q7m"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqhjjm287q7m">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or the fun fact that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6qo3z2ji67g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6qo3z2ji67g">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this function is indeed analytic, since it can be differentiated as often as you want by the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leibniz_integral_rule" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leibniz Rule</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is what the Gamma function looks like over all the real numbers.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, so the positive reals look sensible enough but what the heck is going on with the negative numbers? Is this really what </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> should look like?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, no, it isn't, because it's </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, only a sort of proxy, and that's a point to hold onto for later.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And this is what it looks like over the complex plane, with the height showing the magnitude </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Those peaks being super-sharp is not an error or rendering problem—they are really just that sharp</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So right now, if you're clever, a question might occur to you. I just sort of spat out a definition of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that happens to be analytic and curve-fit the factorial function. Is this the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> possible choice? Is it </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unique</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That would be really nice! Because it would mean that in some concrete sense the Gamma function is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> way to extend the factorial function. Any other choice would be a compromise.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, unfortunately, no. There are infinitely many ways to do it, and the Gamma function is just a convenient choice that everyone likes.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But despair not: there is a case we're about to get to where it </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> unique. You see, if instead of starting with some points on the real number line, you start with a region</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4gwlymejwo3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4gwlymejwo3">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the complex plane and a function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that is analytic on the region, then there is indeed </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only one</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> analytic continuation to the rest of the complex plane.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref977k9yy50ps"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn977k9yy50ps">[12]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then the equals sign is less wobbly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider our original intent: we wanted to find a way to connect points or extend a curve in the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">smoothest</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> way possible. If there's more than one way to do it, then you're arguably just making up your favorite curve, and if z is outside the domain, then you might just be assigning some arbitrary value to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But in the example I just outlined (starting with a region of the complex plane), there is only </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">one</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> way to extend it, which means that the value of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is no longer arbitrary. It's the only possible analytic continuation.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Riemann Zeta Function Enters the Picture</span></h2><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From here on out, for complex numbers of the form </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I'm going to say "real part" for a and "imaginary part" for b and just assume you know what I'm talking about.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, think back to Calc 2: A series </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">converges</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> if it sums to a clear fixed value and </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">diverges</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> if it doesn't. The latter doesn't have to be to go to infinity! A sum can also oscillate back and forth</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nonetheless, it's very common practice to use "diverges" as shorthand for "goes to infinity or negative infinity", which is a practice I will adopt.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now it is time to introduce a very important, very well-known function which matches exactly the description I just gave—a unique analytic continuation of a region of the complex plane—the Riemann Zeta Function.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let us define a precursor function first:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This function is well-defined (the series </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">converges</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to a concrete value) whenever the real part of the exponent </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is greater than 1.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbll9x3996qc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbll9x3996qc">[13]</a></sup></span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Riemann Zeta Function (written </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref075dbf0t6opr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn075dbf0t6opr">[14]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">analytic continuation</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of this function to the rest of the complex plane.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A Tangent: the Riemann Hypothesis</span></h2><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like a (very short-term) fossil in amber, this section was written and edited before Levent Alpöge announced an apparent proof of the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, formalized in Lean. For interest (and because it saves work), this section is mostly untouched, except to add a brief discussion of what makes the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis "General".</span></i></p></blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Amazingly, this section actually has nothing directly to do with the overall topic of the post! But I imagine there is a lot of interest in the Riemann Hypothesis, and we're within spitting distance of it. Honestly, you owe it to yourself to know something about it.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you truly don't care, or already know this material, just skip this section.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is irresponsible to bring up the Riemann Zeta Function without explaining at least briefly what it is and why it's important, so let me go ahead and raise everyone's AI-news–following eyebrows by commenting that the infamous Riemann Hypothesis simply states:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be the Riemann Zeta Function. All values </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (except the trivial set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) have real part </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where I have simplified the mathematical language for understanding.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discussion of the Riemann Hypothesis is a field extremely oversaturated in explainers</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzz56jjtqks"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzz56jjtqks">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, so here is the breezy 100,000-foot explanation of why it's important:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be the prime number counting function, the number of primes less than or equal to x for any real number x. So, for example, for x=8, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, because </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are prime.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">define</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1nerh08cp38"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1nerh08cp38">[16]</a></sup></span></li></ol><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's take a moment to appreciate that if we could exactly calculate </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, this is tantamount to being able to identify every prime number—the prime numbers would just be where </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> increases by 1. So anything we figure out about this function is the same as learning about the primes, with all that entails, like results about cryptography.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also it's confusingly named π and has nothing to do with the number 3.14...</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">for p, because prime numbers, you see. We've inherited </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a lot</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of these conventions from old-timey mathematicians.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_number_theorem" rel="noreferrer"></a><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_number_theorem" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prime number theorem</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9n40qhpwc0n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9n40qhpwc0n">[17]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> we know:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That is, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> gets asymptotically close to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as x goes to infinity.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Top: the prime-counting function π(x) against the approximation Li(x),</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0yl0k2i5ipv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0yl0k2i5ipv">[18]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> up to x = 1000. Bottom: the ratio of the approximation to π(x), out to x = one million. Li(x) is within 0.2% by x = 10⁶ </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Figure generated by Claude with matplotlib; primes by sieve, Li(x) via the exponential integral.)</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is natural to ask whether Li(x) always hovers above π(x) without seeming to approach it, like it appears on the graph. The answer is no: it was proven in 1914 by Littlewood that the two functions cross each other infinitely many times.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa9or9f08dc5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna9or9f08dc5">[19]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So yes, it's an artifact of the graph, but no, showing more numbers or using a log-scale won't help: the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skewes%27s_number" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">first time</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Li(x) (actually li(x)) is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">known</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to drop below π(x) is approximately </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu4khqdo25bk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu4khqdo25bk">[20]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There is no simple graph I could make that shows this well.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9c2sprugrrd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9c2sprugrrd">[21]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On its own, that's neat, but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">awful</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at actually letting us say anything meaningful about the prime numbers.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffov0ka1qck"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfov0ka1qck">[22]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> With more work you can do better. For instance in 1899 it was shown that:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">for some positive constant </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And in 2002 it was shown (hat tip to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_number_theorem#Prime-counting_function_in_terms_of_the_logarithmic_integral" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wikipedia</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), deep breath:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But wouldn't it be great if we could do better? Like a lot better?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, if the Riemann Hypothesis were true, then:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and, dispensing with the big-O notation entirely (still assuming the RH):</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where we define </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefl9ny1eo1z09"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnl9ny1eo1z09">[23]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So the Riemann Hypothesis places much stricter bounds on the distribution of prime numbers, with a whole laundry list of theorems about primes and other functions dependent on it being </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riemann_hypothesis#Consequences" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">true</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and it has resisted proof attempts since 1859. Computer searches have ruled out counterexamples (a zero</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbyvq5bx7y98"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbyvq5bx7y98">[24]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with real part  </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) with imaginary part less than about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, finding over ten trillion zeros with real part one-half.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbedcb897ckk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbedcb897ckk">[25]</a></sup></span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Generalized Riemann Hypothesis </span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because mathematicians were not satisfied with just the Riemann Hypothesis, it was eventually noticed that the curious property that seems to hold for the Riemann Zeta Function (all non-trivial zeroes have real part 1/2) also seems to hold for a whole family of related functions, the primitive </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirichlet_L-function?wprov=sfla1" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dirichlet L-functions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And like the Standard Riemann Hypothesis, the generalized version has significant consequences for number theory, for instance in guaranteeing runtimes for a number of prime-related algorithms.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is no getting away from the math here. If you want to know the full extent of what Levent/Claude proved I have to engage in the deep arts. Feel free to skip if you don't want to know the details. </span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you'll recall, we defined the Riemann Zeta Function as the analytic continuation of: </span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">beyond its original definition on complex numbers with real part greater than 1. In comparison, a Dirichlet L-function replaces the numerator of 1 with a Dirichlet Character, which is a function defined as such: </span></p><div></div><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">keeping in mind that n and q are integers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a L-function to be primitive, its character function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> must not be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">induced</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (a formal term for "generated") by another character </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with smaller modulus. That is, there is no character </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with modulus </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> such that: </span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the greatest common divisor function (so if it equals 1, n and q share no factor). (d also necessarily divides q, as a consequence of this definition). If there was such a character, that would</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">make the original character function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> nearly identical to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, just with extra zeroes whenever n and q share a common factor, and therefore not "primitive".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With these characteristics defining the L-function, then:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Riemann Zeta Function is a special case with trivial character 1.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The function always converges if the real part of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is greater than 1.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffvp93o9cebs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfvp93o9cebs">[26]</a></sup></span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we now take the analytic continuation to the rest of complex plane, we get the full Dirichlet L-function.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It can be shown that any such function is either zero at all the negative even numbers or the negative odd numbers. These are the trivial zeroes. The Generalized Riemann Hypothesis is then: </span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For all primitive Dirichlet L-functions, all non-trivial zeroes have real part 1/2</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Levent's Lean proof, it seems that this (and the Riemann Hypothesis itself) are true.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Back to the Main Topic: The Riemann Zeta Function and 1+2+3...</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Back to the series we came here for.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leaving aside why this has anything to do with the prime numbers, as discussed already the Riemann Zeta Function is defined </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">starting with</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the infinite sum:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">which only converges if s</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhd2q2jjel8k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhd2q2jjel8k">[27]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has real part &gt;1.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5wcula2s9p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5wcula2s9p">[28]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To get the actual Zeta Function we </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Analytically Continue</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this function to the rest of the complex plane, and as discussed at the end of the Analytic Continuation subsection, since we started with a region of the complex plane, this function is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unique</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. There is no ambiguity about what analytic continuation we are talking about.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Actually </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">calculating</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> values of the Zeta Function gets a little tricky. For values with real part &gt;1 some exact values are known, e.g. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4xryz3fvoke"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4xryz3fvoke">[29]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the rest can be numerically approximated by computers. For values with real part below zero, we rely on this rather horrifying relation, which was shown by Riemann himself:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is our good friend the Gamma Function from last section. This relates negative values of s to corresponding positive values. For instance if s=-1, then 1-s=2 and we can use </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to calculate </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This leaves a notable gap between real part 0 and 1 where this doesn't quite work, but which can be closed by a different trick that I'm going to leave out here.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3at6p26oxkl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3at6p26oxkl">[30]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some fun facts about the Riemann Zeta Function:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It remains undefined at s=1, even after analytic continuation. Note that this is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the real number 1; 1 + i is perfectly fine.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is zero if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which are the only zeros on the real number line. The complex zeros are the subject of the Riemann Hypothesis. The zeros on the real number line are fully understood.</span></li><li value="3"><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is, let's see... </span><span class="math-tex"></span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hey wait a second—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, assuming you didn't see it already, if you pretend that the Riemann Zeta function is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">literally</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, then if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, this sum is just </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And as we just established, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">so:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in the same sense that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "equals" </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By the same logic, you can also say:</span></p><div></div><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and infinitely many other such statements. You can go ahead and imagine air quotes around all these equals signs.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the takeaway is...</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I imagine at this point this is a little underwhelming. Of course, we knew the "equals sign" wasn't a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">real</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> equals sign, but what does this say, really? Sure this happens to involve the Riemann Zeta Function, which is a cool function that's important to math, but the cool prime number part seems to have nothing to do with this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, yeah, that was the objection a lot of people—mathematicians included—had to this "fun fact". It seemed like a bit of a parlor trick. As we shall see, it is somehow more than that.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If at this point you are drowning and want to get to the "applications" already, feel free to skip to Section 3. You may want to go back to Section 2 afterward to see the connections, though.</span></i></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2 - "Smooth Sums" of Sequences and Why -1/12 is Special</span></b></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Speaking very loosely, when looking for alternative ways to attempt to "evaluate" divergent summations, such as with </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramanujan_summation" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ramanujan Summation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the answer that usually comes out is a match for the series above. That is:</span></p><div></div><div></div><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, it is possible to come up with various more or less suspicious schemes for messing with sequences that will also have a tendency to land in roughly the same place. This seems like a strange but probably meaningless coincidence, except, well, it isn't. And we don't even have to resort to the complex numbers to explain it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflcocwhntyfd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlcocwhntyfd">[31]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2010 (and probably earlier), Terence Tao asked the question: what if we evaluated sums a different way? That is, the "traditional" (and obvious) way to try to evaluate an infinite sum is with partial sums:</span></p><div></div><div></div><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But what if we instead took a weighted sum? That is, rather than cutting the infinite series off and focusing only on a limited part of the beginning, we took the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whole</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> series, but tried to downweight the later parts and bring them in only gradually?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let me do a toy example with the simple series </span><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, instead of summing terms one at a time, we chop off the later numbers to "make" the series converge, then try to fix it by putting values back in. As a simple example, let's make each subsequent term only </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">half</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as important as the previous term:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where we're "down-weighting" each subsequent term </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We sort of chopped the series horizontally rather than vertically. And in this case we know this chopped series sums to 2. That's a real equals sign! Now to get back to the original series, let's bring the missing parts back piece by piece. We'll need a smaller piece to add back, so let's down-weight by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this time, rather than by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg8ufd4w132s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng8ufd4w132s">[32]</a></sup></span></p><div></div><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can keep doing this manually, but it's easier to generalize a procedure. We can look at the series </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "chopped" series:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">which should</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6sxdleq4ufq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6sxdleq4ufq">[33]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> approach the same value. And here we see that each subsum is:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">which indeed approaches infinity. As indeed, it really should.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is... certainly one way to sum a series, but why are we doing this again?</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bear with me a bit longer. I was mostly using that as an example to ease us into the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actual</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> procedure that is used:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Define the weighting function (the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">smooth cutoff</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> function, to use the right name for it) as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We require that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, that the function be smooth on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and that the function evaluate to exactly 0 outside a certain finite region.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6w4sj9mhopn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6w4sj9mhopn">[34]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> These are the criteria that define a suitable cutoff function, and they are sufficient to guarantee the series converges (because, well, it becomes 0 at some point).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp2y21tufk7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp2y21tufk7">[35]</a></sup></span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then we define the series of smooth sums:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the original series we were interested in, although Tao's result focuses on series of the form </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj6c5wqfzyn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj6c5wqfzyn">[36]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> . </span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Confusingly, while Tao sticks to Zeta Function convention by talking about the series </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at points in his post, he uses the opposite convention in his formula, with the implicit definition of </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. To keep the formulas the same as in his post I'll stick to the latter.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that this is just flipping the sign of s. So for </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we would have s=-1 here, but it corresponds to </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; that is: </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Terence Tao showed the surprising fact that:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the value predicted by analytic continuation and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Or in other words, the series of smooth sums is equal to:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or, in other words, the value B is baked into what you get no matter what you choose for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as long as it "looks like" what we defined as a cutoff function. It's a property of the original series, and it also means that various "schemes" for shuffling terms around to get rid of the infinities will have a strong tendency to spit out B at the end.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thus, in a way, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> really is secretly implied by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, without needing to invoke complex numbers or analytic continuation. </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OK so uh, that's great. This is a nice fun abstract math fact. Can we go home now—</span></i></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3 - Quantum Field Theory, Regularization, and the Vacuum Energy</span></b></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay wait what? How did we get here?</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alright, so this is the part of the post where I must do absolutely the most hand-waving, partly because not hand-waving involves a dive into QFT, and also because, frankly, I don't know QFT that well.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As you may or may not have heard, when formulating Quantum Field Theories, trying to calculate values for simple concepts (like the mass of the electron) very quickly veers into the rocks of divergent integrals and summations. Two common ways this can occur are:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Performing calculations that involve very high energies or, equivalently</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjtt2kkg1ywg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjtt2kkg1ywg">[37]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, very small distances.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Performing calculations that require you to consider an infinite number of increasingly convoluted particle interactions as part of the probability distribution. For instance, an electron, emitting a virtual photon, that splits into a virtual electron and positron, each of which emits a virtual photon...</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is not, as commonly supposed, that classical physics is free of such problems. For instance, performing electromagnetic calculations involving point-like electrons necessarily involves an infinite electric field at the "surface" of the electron and tacitly involves assuming the electron isn't "really" a point or that we don't care about such small distances. It's just that in classical physics, this pretty much never mattered—but in quantum physics it absolutely does.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reflecting on that point about classical physics (that we were basically "ignoring" things that were too small) motivates "fixing" these problems in </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">very </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">roughly the following way</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmxj4hs6luyf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmxj4hs6luyf">[38]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regularization</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose we are trying to calculate A, which has the property that a direct QFT calculation yields:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Except that unfortunately trying to evaluate this series diverges to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (for example, if the function x(n) is something like just </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, wait, it turns out that as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the energy scale goes to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the length scale goes to 0. This is exactly the same thing as the "infinitely small electron" problem. Analogous to what we implicitly did in classical physics, we can just say we sort of don't believe in the higher energy parts, and replace our previous equation with something like:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is some function that starts off at 1 and drops off to 0 eventually, and N is chosen to "cut off" the terms somewhere around where we stop believing our physics is accurate. If the terms of x(n) drop off sufficiently rapidly, this won't even be that bad an estimate.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This should look familiar at this point. This is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">very similar</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to what was described in the last section.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This procedure (and others like it) is used in real quantum mechanics to make real (extremely accurate) quantitative predictions, and is worth our attention as mathematicians and physicists because real quantum mechanics has to try to deal with divergent series and make them work, somehow.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And to illustrate this, we should talk about the Casimir Effect.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Casimir Effect</span></h3><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like so many topics here, I am giving only a surface-level treatment.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A bare-minimum description of the Casimir Effect is that it is a physical force that arises in confined spaces due solely to quantum fluctuations in a vacuum. Just saying those words is a bit mysterious, but the classical example from Casimir himself should give some of the requisite flavor:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793170445/lexical_client_uploads/mygd1luon7pj09ahotrq.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Image from </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_effect" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wikipedia</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before getting to the two (conducting!) plates shown above, some surprising predictions of Quantum Field Theory on an empty vacuum:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even empty space is filled with random oscillating fluctuations of the various quantum fields, essentially because the Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle forbids a field from being simultaneously zero energy and zero rate of change.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The average energy of the vacuum, the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">vacuum energy</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, is non-zero.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These fluctuations are quantized—that is, they only happen in multiples of a discrete value.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An attempt to estimate the vacuum energy mentioned in point 2 immediately runs into a familiar problem: infinite fluctuations at infinite points form a sum that</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">diverges to infinity. Fortunately for most calculations, only </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">differences</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the vacuum energy usually matter; for most physical systems you might be studying, the vacuum energy everywhere should push all things equally and cancel out.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbbl980vdupi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbbl980vdupi">[39]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Though</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> not for the universe as a whole</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The negative pressure of the energy actually exceeds the gravitational pull, and an infinite vacuum energy would blow up the universe immediately. This is foreshadowing.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now let's return to the two conducting plates Casimir suggested. Speaking roughly, if the plates are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really close</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> together, then the quantized nature of the vacuum fluctuations (prediction 3 earlier) starts to matter.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specifically, for the electromagnetic field</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqkl74jzyxsd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqkl74jzyxsd">[40]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, quantization starts to limit the range of possible electromagnetic wavelengths, because the waves have to "fit" between the plates, because the field strength at the surface of the conducting plates needs to be 0.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefym0wgtsutii"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnym0wgtsutii">[41]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This reduction in the number of possible oscillations between the plates </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lowers the vacuum energy</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, because it reduces the number of possible random fluctuations, sort of like switching from a D20 to a D6.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this means that there is a positive pressure from the vacuum energy outside the plates (still using a D20) pushing the plates inward, which seems like an experimental check on whether this kooky vacuum energy really exists.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflexrn1pr6qg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlexrn1pr6qg">[42]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Naturally, this has been measured, and the measurements seem to agree with the calculated theoretical values.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflhlk9c10r8c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlhlk9c10r8c">[43]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That theoretical calculation is what we are here to talk about. Casimir originally calculated that the energy per unit area between the plates from this effect should be the following value:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the distance between the plates, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the reduced Planck's constant</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0fua8m8sxqav"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0fua8m8sxqav">[44]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the the speed of light.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And we need to take the limit as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. That summation on the right side? It is a divergent sum </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of the same form</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as we were using to define the Riemann Zeta Function.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi4mcp9c3rnb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni4mcp9c3rnb">[45]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The typical treatment then proceeds by going OK, let's replace this with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the zeta function analytic continuation, and proceed to calculate the result, deriving an energy per unit area, and from it a pressure pushing the two plates together of: </span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">something which has been roughly experimentally confirmed, as mentioned previously. The two plates just sitting next to each other really do get pressured together.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But wait, isn't that absolutely bonkers? We're just waving a seemingly magic wand over the original formula, asserting that:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmnrjkor95qg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmnrjkor95qg">[46]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this hack predicts real experimental results???</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, it is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">kind of</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> bonkers, but it makes more sense than it seems. In his original derivation in 1948, Casimir didn't take this approach; he performed </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">regularization</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, assuming that the higher frequencies were less important. He defined a cutoff function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, specifying that it would start from 1 then drop off to zero rapidly.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv3xcrwhnvmh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv3xcrwhnvmh">[47]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> He himself considered the choice of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> arbitrary and a bit of mathematical convenience.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Along the way, he canceled out an equivalent expression for "infinitely distant" plates, effectively subtracting off the infinite components of the vacuum energy. By doing this he got the same formula as the Riemann Zeta Function.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the time and for some time afterward, this looked a little mysterious, even if it worked, but with our result from Section 2, it is much less so. In fact, Section 2 implies it would have worked with any smooth cutoff choice—the important thing is that there is a cutoff at all. We may not understand exactly what it is or how it should be shaped, but the higher frequencies/smaller distances are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">somehow</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> less important.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, from section 2 we know:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is of the form </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is a smooth cutoff function. So then:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When Casimir subtracted off his infinitely distant plates term, he effectively subtracted off the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">middle part</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only part that goes to infinity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefis6ic5hzoe"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnis6ic5hzoe">[48]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This naturally just leaves:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this is just </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note the key point here: the results of Section 2 (the discussion on smooth cutoffs) took a somewhat shaky physics calculation and put it on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">firmer epistemological</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ground—rather than needing to accept </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as an arbitrary choice that happens to work, the assumption is clearer: the higher frequency/energy components are less important (for some reason we don't know yet), and get cut off in the real world </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">somehow</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We bypass the question of exactly how with this one neat mathematical trick that lets us dodge the lack of understanding that our formulas otherwise carry with them. The wrinkle is, of course, we're still merely assuming they </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> get cut off in the real world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, in the case of the Casimir effect we've actually managed to eventually develop the relevant understanding: physicists are now quite sure that the cutoff is derived from the plasma frequency of the metal, and that frequencies higher than the cutoff can simply pass through the metal, ignoring the plates. That means above-cutoff electromagnetic radiation </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> contribute to vacuum energy between the two Casimir plates, which means our formula was correct when it "arbitrarily" chose to exclude those higher frequencies from its count of missing vacuum energy between the plates. A theoretically perfect metal would not have this cutoff, but all real plates do. And perhaps that's the real lesson here.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Widget made by Claude and redesigned by Gemini Neon, calculating the Casimir pressure between plates taking the metal's imperfect conductivity into account. The "ideal" pressure is that calculated from Casimir's theory. The "corrected" pressure introduces a first-order correction from the plasma theory of metals,</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6r85q40yd9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6r85q40yd9">[49]</a></sup></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which is trustworthy when a ≳ 2.5 λ</span></i><sub><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">p.</span></i></sub><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This allows us to compare Casimir's result with a close to real pressure (within 10-25% at typical experimental separations).</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The key observation: A real metal confines only the waves with wavelength above its plasma wavelength (blue waves on the chart), while letting other waves (grey on the chart) through. As you take the plasma wavelength </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0 (the metal becomes a better and better conductor), more and more waves are confined and the pressure approaches Casimir's estimate. BUT: the cancellation term, which is canceled out when constructing the theory, blows up to infinity.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffc088hjtp2i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfc088hjtp2i">[50]</a></sup></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For an ideal metal, this term </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> infinity, and subtracting it is technically invalid</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">but that didn't stop Casimir from getting a real answer anyway by cutting off the higher frequencies.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a real example, gold has λ</span></i><sub><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">p</span></i></sub><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ≈ 138 nm. Atmospheric pressure is about 10</span></i><sup><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5 </span></i></sup><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pa, so even at 100 nm plate separation the Casimir pressure is about 0.01% of atmospheric pressure.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So it's great that we have this relatively well-understood case study in why cutoffs work. But the thing is, we also use cutoffs elsewhere in physics, where it remains </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">much less obvious</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> what the physical meaning of those cutoffs are.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Black Hole Thermodynamics</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Casimir Effect is the most famous</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa5jadbdkiiu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna5jadbdkiiu">[51]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> use of what is called </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeta_function_regularization" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zeta Function Regularization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and nowadays the relevant formula is typically derived this way rather than the way Casimir originally did it. But it is not the original reason the technique was introduced.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You do not need to follow the math closely in this section! The important part is the written explanation of how the Zeta Function Regularization we just learned about ended up being accepted as a useful tool in theoretical physics. </span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the 1970s, physicists</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyhbx8i6llq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyhbx8i6llq">[52]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> began examining the notion of using zeta function regularization to study the "determinants of quantum operators", which are important in certain calculations but have the unfortunate property of often requiring you to take the determinant of an operator on an infinite-dimensional </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilbert_space" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hilbert Space</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvpzz8dsvw4s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvpzz8dsvw4s">[53]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This necessarily requires an infinitely long calculation. However, many key quantum operators (momentum, position, spin, etc.) are </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-adjoint_operator" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">self-adjoint</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, meaning that (among other things) each one has a full set of eigenvalues that fully characterize it. The </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">spectral zeta function </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of the operator A is then defined as the infinite sum:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where the zeta symbol is reused, possibly, to confuse the non-expert reader as much as possible. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> functional determinant</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqpf2kpdo2n9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqpf2kpdo2n9">[54]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, then, is defined as:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhj21gr5s9w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhj21gr5s9w">[55]</a></sup></span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, the sum above is not identical to the sum in the Zeta Function (which necessarily goes 1, 2, 3... unlike here), but it's close enough that the same analytic continuation procedure etc. is used. </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In particular, Dowker and Critchley pointed out in 1976 that this could potentially be used to evaluate otherwise divergent path integrals on a Riemannian Manifold</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk9grsa6yz18"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk9grsa6yz18">[56]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> without the downsides of other approaches (like violating tenets of General Relativity).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 1977, Stephen Hawking showed that:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In flat space-time, we know the eigenvalues exactly, so we can calculate the determinant explicitly (it still diverges).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a fixed, curved space-time, we have to be more clever, and Hawking resorts to a "heat kernel" approximation that cuts off terms corresponding to short periods of time.</span><ol><li value="1"><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can't be calculated explicitly, but </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can, and ends up controlling a key term.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unlike other approaches to regularization,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdu18jutvsxh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndu18jutvsxh">[57]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this satisfies </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_covariance" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">General Covariance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a core tenet of General Relativity, so we can be more confident in the approach in this context.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In particular, the one-loop quantum correction to the path integral partition function Z can be written:</span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...and at this point we are definitely delving far too greedily and too deep, and beyond where I can confidently narrate the math, so I will sum up by saying:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hawking was able to use this to perform his analysis of space-time around a black hole.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmydppl06uo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmydppl06uo">[58]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Among other things, this provides a number of the one-loop correction terms for the black hole partition function, which in turn provides corrections to the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_hole_thermodynamics" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bekenstein–Hawking Entropy of a Black Hole</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> beyond the classical </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> term.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of this served to popularize the notion of using Zeta Function Regularization in practical physics work, including the normal variation used in the Casimir effect. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before this</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, it was considered borderline mathematical voodoo, but Hawking's success with it in Black Hole calculations caused the physics community to pay attention.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It took decades of development in </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effective_field_theory" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effective Field Theory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to fully reason out the connection between this approach and the usual approach of "cutting off" higher energy modes. Tao's method in Section 2 </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">works here too</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, for much the same reason, and makes the relationship explicit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It turns out once again, we are completing our calculations by removing the highest energy terms. But unlike with the Casimir Effect, it is no longer clear what exactly we are removing. Yes, technically, Hawking was removing the shortest timescale effects that don't have time to "propagate"—but if these were truly trivially unimportant, the sum wouldn't diverge! Taking them out is saying something: maybe that we don't think they really happen, or something changes about how they work, space-time doesn't permit it, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">something</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What would be even worse would be a case where we don't even know where to set the cutoff.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vacuum Energy, and the Cosmological Constant</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our last topic is one we touched upon earlier when discussing the Casimir Effect: the Vacuum Energy—that is, the energy associated with the quantum fields of empty space.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As noted earlier, actually attempting to calculate the value of this energy from the Standard Model leads to a divergent sum. In many calculations this value does not matter, since it cancels out or is irrelevant, but on a universal scale it </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">absolutely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> matters, because it is expected to contribute to the expansion or contraction of the universe. In particular, it is by far the leading candidate for the origin of the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmological_constant" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cosmological Constant</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which seems to be driving the accelerating expansion of the universe, essentially by applying a constant pressure outward.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No problem, you might say, we'll just use the techniques of regularization and cut off the higher energy terms!
