We just learned how and why to do that!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, the thing is, in dealing with vacuum energy outside the Casimir Effect (where we could cancel the infinities out against each other), the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">remaining</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> terms do matter, and depend on exactly how we cut it off. In many places in Quantum Field Theory (and in Hawking's approach above), choosing a rough cutoff parameter that clearly includes the energy and momentum scale of the problem being studied (but excludes anything "too large") works quite well. For instance, when studying the electron—mass-energy 0.511 MeV</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnsqk46y2k5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnsqk46y2k5">[59]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Joules—a choice of cutoff a thousand times its mass-energy shifts the mass calculation by about 2.5%. A choice at the Planck Energy (about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the electron energy) shifts it by 18%. Here, the answer is nearly insensitive to any reasonable cutoff, and physicists are comfortable using renormalization</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk0kgh0bw4zh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk0kgh0bw4zh">[60]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to adjust the parameter to the experimental mass.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the Vacuum energy, it's less obvious what the best choice is, and the result is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">extremely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> sensitive to the choice of cutoff </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, meaning that the energy is proportional to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Various choices based on intuitive physical parameters (such as the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck_units" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Planck Energy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) give results that are incredibly, absurdly out-of-line with observations of the Cosmological Constant, by factors of as much as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefok5yj0ua1rm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnok5yj0ua1rm">[61]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is known, relatively famously, as the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmological_constant_problem" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cosmological Constant Problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For practical calculations where it does matter, we can </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pick</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a cutoff value to get the number about right, analogous to renormalization, but, to quote Wikipedia, "many theorists consider this equivalent to ignoring the problem", a view I share.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So here, in one of the more important unsolved problems in physics, is a divergent sum we can't figure out how to handle. Zeta Function Regularization has helped before, but will it help here? Well, I doubt it will be as simple as that, or as simple as knowing that 1+2+3+4+5...=-1/12</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzksg9xyp3cr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzksg9xyp3cr">[62]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but understanding just what's going on here may be part of the answer.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a final thought, it's not a coincidence that so many physicists are convinced that these cutoff points (the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck_units" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Planck length, energy, etc.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) must be the starting point of actual new physics, whether it be String Theory, Loop Quantum Gravity, or what have you. The Casimir effect with its concrete arguments about metal plates is the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">good</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> scenario—there are many, many cases where it's not at all clear what we're cutting off and why.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs75bh54352"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns75bh54352">[63]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A lot of these prospective new theories share the advantage of giving an answer to some of these questions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We just don't know yet if they're right.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With acknowledgments to Julian Bradshaw for invaluable editing work and commentary, Claude for proofreading, widgets, and fact-checking, and Gemini Neon for reviewing the widgets and writing several of their captions.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp50q8mpp8l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp50q8mpp8l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For instance, the </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w-I6XTVZXww" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Numberphile video </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which attracted a lot of controversy when it was first uploaded. The thing is, "shuffling sequences around" </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a lot more meaningful than it might seem at first glance, but in very non-obvious ways, which we discuss in Section 2. Note that Numberphile much later </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=beakj767uG4" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">returned</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to also explain the argument in Section 2 and</span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FmLIGN8ZGdw" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the argument</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in Section 1, and these videos are fine.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0efalm4i6les"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0efalm4i6les">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How impure</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncpd7zx1vyga"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcpd7zx1vyga">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2dmhf1u57he">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Straight from the mouth of the horse himself, </span><a href="https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2010/04/10/the-euler-maclaurin-formula-bernoulli-numbers-the-zeta-function-and-real-variable-analytic-continuation/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Terence Tao</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncl8oqzycmxt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcl8oqzycmxt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By the way, the first person to derive the formula </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">might have been</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Euler, but whether he actually did or not is surprisingly disputed. As a non-Euler scholar, I'm going to stay out of this one.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4f1fweunwxq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4f1fweunwxq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(deep sigh), so OK technically, this is a function that is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">holomorphic. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technically, to be analytic, a function must equal a convergent power series at all points in the domain—basically, its Taylor series. For complex functions, where z and f(z) are allowed to be complex-valued, holomorphic and analytic are identical, which makes a lot of sense. For real functions, it is possible to have infinitely differentiable functions that are not analytic—technically these are called smooth, rather than holomorphic, which technically only applies to complex-value functions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is part of a global theme where math is consistent and behaves how you'd expect on the complex plane, and way messier and filled with edge cases if you focus on only the reals.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anyway, as we will very soon be focusing primarily on the complex plane, I will keep just using the word "analytic", because it matches "analytic continuation".</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnys6xg4pubgi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefys6xg4pubgi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This also implies the function and all its derivatives are continuous.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4ulaxhkmsdf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4ulaxhkmsdf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apparently we have very few portraits of the guy. But this is the smallest crossing point that has been proven to exist.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9c2sprugrrd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9c2sprugrrd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Among other things, at that point the ratio between the two values is so close to 1 that even on a log-log chart the two lines will look pixel-identical.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfov0ka1qck"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffov0ka1qck">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was asked to explain the importance of understanding the prime numbers. See </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geometric_series" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Geometric series</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6sxdleq4ufq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a I didn't choose these, these are by convention!</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjtt2kkg1ywg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjtt2kkg1ywg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmxj4hs6luyf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmxj4hs6luyf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Just Regularization</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is actually insufficient to deal with all our infinities. Typically, it also requires a second procedure in parallel: Renormalization.</span></p><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renormalization" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Renormalization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is arguably even battier than Regularization and kind of goes: OK so I see we're trying to calculate (Something that diverges). But if we pretend everything is fine and rearrange a little we get something like [function of thing we can measure in the lab, like electron mass] + [something that diverges] - [same thing that diverges], which we'll just say is equal to [function of thing we can measure in the lab, like electron mass]. Yes we just did </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but look the expressions subtracted algebraically, the theory works, and we don't think the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is real, in the same way that no one really thought a point-electron has an infinite electric field. If the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">isn't</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> real, then the two mathematically identical things should be okay to subtract. It also hinges on the fact that we just measure and assign a value to the first part.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbbl980vdupi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbbl980vdupi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This assumption is itself a form of renormalization.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqkl74jzyxsd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqkl74jzyxsd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quantum Electrodynamics, if you insist</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnym0wgtsutii"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefym0wgtsutii">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More precisely, any electric field that arises parallel to the surface of the plate will be automatically canceled out by charges moving around inside the conductor (e.g. electrons moving towards or away the field). Thus, the only EM waves that can form will have a field strength of 0 at the surfaces, and quantization restricts how many different possible waves can do this. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that this leans on the assumption of the plates being perfect conductors. The corrections necessary for a real conductor have also been calculated.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlexrn1pr6qg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflexrn1pr6qg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fair warning: not everyone agrees with this. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/hep-th/0503158" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jaffe (2005)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> argued that the Casimir effect can be computed entirely from the interactions between the charges in the plates, with no mention of vacuum energy. The vacuum energy calculation is the standard textbook route and gives the right answer, and most physicists still describe the effect that way, but actually “proving” the existence of the vacuum energy takes more than just showing the Casimir effect</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlhlk9c10r8c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflhlk9c10r8c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See for instance </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_effect#Measurement" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0fua8m8sxqav"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0fua8m8sxqav">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is Planck's constant. The reduced version turns up frequently in calculations and formulas.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fni4mcp9c3rnb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi4mcp9c3rnb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reminder: </span><span class="math-tex"></span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmnrjkor95qg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmnrjkor95qg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You might be slightly surpassed to see </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, unlike every other example that has come up so far, but this is a consequence of the formula from section 1 (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), because of the sine term. The negative even integers are 0 and the negative odd integers alternate between positive and negative.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv3xcrwhnvmh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv3xcrwhnvmh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For instance, we could use the commonly used </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technical note: previously I stipulated that the function needs to drop off to 0 eventually ("compactly supported") but neither Casimir's formula nor the common </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> style function </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually reaches</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 0. With </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the constant term is   </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is what Casimir's calculation finds"</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnis6ic5hzoe"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefis6ic5hzoe">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reminder: this is still the technically forbidden </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but is generally accepted in renormalization.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6r85q40yd9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6r85q40yd9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specifically, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> F ≈ F</span></i><sub><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ideal</span></i></sub><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(1 − 16δ/3a) with δ = λ</span></i><sub><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">p</span></i></sub><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">/2π.</span></i></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfc088hjtp2i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffc088hjtp2i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The exact size of the cancellation term shown in the widget depends on how you model the cutoff. Each MeV is about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Joules.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk0kgh0bw4zh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk0kgh0bw4zh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See Footnote 38</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnok5yj0ua1rm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefok5yj0ua1rm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Implying a universe that blew itself apart long ago. Also a vacuum energy that is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the mass-energy density of steel.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzksg9xyp3cr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzksg9xyp3cr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Honorable mention: In Bosonic String Theory, this exact relation is apparently responsible for why the theory needs 26 dimensions</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns75bh54352"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs75bh54352">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I haven't even brought up attempts to quantize gravity, which fail to renormalize entirely. This can only be explained by handwave: but roughly it is because the gravitational potential is based on energy, rather than a charge that is a pure number. When attempting to renormalize, you integrate over possible energies and set an energy cutoff </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For all quantum field theories, this turns out to hit an edge case in our regularization scheme: remember, the constant term leftover is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this is undefined if s is -1</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (the sum </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). For the better behaved theories, this unknown value can be set in some cases by renormalization by comparison with measured values of the electron mass or charge or other relevant known physical quantities. But for gravity, the fact that the terms involve energy squared and no unitless "charges" means that there are an infinite number of unknown values that need to be arbitrarily set. Obviously, this leaves too many degrees of freedom open to say anything meaningful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Notably, Fermi's original theory of the Weak Nuclear Force had the same problem, which was eventually fixed by new physics in the electroweak theory and the W boson, which changed how the electroweak field potentials are calculated. Suggestive.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/F7DNBdoADqktjn2Hk/understanding-why-1-2-3-4-5-1-12-rather-than-infinity#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Reflections on 37 weeks of hosting EA/LW/ACX board games](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/y8K3FREw4R7me99ib/reflections-on-37-weeks-of-hosting-ea-lw-acx-board-games)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Epistemic Status: Personal experience, supplemented with good, though slightly flawed, data)</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the start of 2025, I moved across the country to a city (Boston) I knew little about and that was filled entirely with people I didn’t know. Nearly two years later, I’m now happier, more satisfied, and have a better social life than ever before, and it is basically all downstream of deciding, at the start of 2026, that I was going to start hosting board games. After successfully hosting an initial board game night and querying attendees on continued interest, I committed myself to hosting every week until people lost interest or I was otherwise prevented from continuing. More than 9 months later, I am very happy to say that I am still hosting (approximately) every week (I’ve hosted ~37 out of the 45 weeks).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In terms of specific life-improvements, I think that hosting board games has been directly and obviously causal in meeting my amazing girlfriend, turning several casual acquaintances into best friends, and generally increasing my average happiness by ~2-3 points (on a 10-point scale). I also just know a lot more people in the area, feel like I’m part of a community, and get to attend a lot more engaging and novel events with people I find interesting.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795706337/lexical_client_uploads/b5jbvqtp97rzrl2dra6w.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are the </span></i><a href="https://partiful.com/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Partiful</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> RSVP responses for individual board game nights so far. I expect these are often underestimates since I have two consistent attendees who refuse to RSVP beforehand and a few who have occasionally forgotten to RSVP. One night (Sat #33) is excluded from the graph since nobody RSVP’d, even though two people attended.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795706337/lexical_client_uploads/mhyswyc8nhxocuflpiyo.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">‘Going’ RSVPs, aggregated by month. Underestimates attendance from ‘Maybe’ RSVPs, but gives a general estimate.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given my now fairly substantial experience hosting, I figured it was worthwhile for me to write up some of my reflections on hosting, so here they are (in proper </span><a href="https://dynomight.net/lists/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">list form</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hosting makes attending events simple and easy. Before I started hosting, I regularly had the experience of thinking it would be good to go out and attend a specific event, but ultimately backing out since it was easy to just skip. It also substantially reduces transport time for obvious reasons.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effective Altruist, LessWrong, and Astral Codex Ten community members are substantially more interesting and enjoyable to spend time with than ~any other people I have met (though I am filtering on various axes, including “enjoys the events I host”). I think it would be hard to overstate the value of shared cognitive styles and shared culture among these communities for making strong connections.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It can be difficult to predict who will be a regular attendee. I can think of at least three very regular attendees who I assumed would not show up again after their first event.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keeping attendance open and explicitly welcoming first-time attendees creates a nice flow of new people, which keeps events more interesting and helps prevent devolution into stale in-jokes. It is also valuable since there is some attrition on existing attendees that has to be made up for.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hosting in a home is often much better than a public place, especially for board games. In particular, the general noise levels are much lower and the seating, etc. are more comfortable/manipulable. I don’t play music when hosting and I think this is preferable since it lets people hear each other more easily. That said, I expect there is a trade-off here with welcoming new people since individuals are likely more hesitant to attend a non-public-venue event if they don’t already know other attendees. This has been addressed in my case by the fact that the Boston community very often has other (public-venue) events. It is also just actually the case that there are a surprising number of people who are willing to show up at my board game events without knowing anyone prior (I think this is great!).</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New people appearing/attending is generally fairly difficult to predict since they are often people who are only recently in town or only recently heard about the events. Relatedly, it is often difficult to predict how people hear about events.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You generally get fewer attendees the later you announce an event (on the timescale of a week). This is mitigated by the fact that I host approximately every week so people anticipate and plan around it even when I forget to send out invites/event postings until late in the week.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many people will come once and seem to have a good time and then never appear again. My RSVP data shows that approximately 29 of the 53 unique attendees (55%) only came once (not including anonymous/unnamed attendees).</span></li></ul><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795706337/lexical_client_uploads/hzuzhhd25q5rjewwxhau.png" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795706337/lexical_client_uploads/csyexwnmwmfeqdu5w133.png" /><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regular attendees make up most of the total attendance over time. Specifically, 7 regular attendees (those with 10+ events) make up 55% of all measured attendance (among named ‘Going’ RSVPs). My measured attendance for board games has an average/median around 5 or 6. Given that I have a couple regular attendees who don’t RSVP, I think this is probably closer to 6 or 7.</span></li></ul><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795706337/lexical_client_uploads/ptcdqfqdazizrapgz3u2.png" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795706337/lexical_client_uploads/gnslbrau162egbbpsbfx.png" /><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People in the community enjoy mechanically dense games, but most still actively enjoy silly and/or relatively simple games, especially when they are more social.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is valuable to have “starter” board games to play at the beginning of the night that can easily accommodate additional players as they arrive. Great examples of this include </span><a href="https://boardgamegeek.com/boardgame/312786/poetry-for-neanderthals"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Poetry for Neanderthals</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://boardgamegeek.com/boardgame/445610/faekin"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fae Kin</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but generally anything that is fast and can support a wide range of players is probably good here.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maintaining novelty is very important when hosting on a regular basis. Simple methods for achieving novelty mainly include new/different board games and new/different people. In order to maintain novelty for regulars, it is best to have some rare or entirely different events (e.g. reading groups, kayaking, singalongs, hikes) that push people out of the social grooves that they have been etching.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hosting is helpful for keeping up a social life if you (hypothetically) end up breaking your ankle backpacking in Vermont (this is an important rite of passage for Boston community organizers).</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are occasionally disruptive people that I have had to eject in some fashion (3 so far, plus 1 that I would have uninvited if he showed up again). There’s a lot that could be said about this kind of thing, but my main takeaways would be (1) this has been a relatively minor part of my experience (I really don’t think about it much) and (2) this can be very simple and just consist of messaging them when they RSVP and requesting that they don’t attend (I don’t generally provide specific reasons since I’m not interested in litigating my justifications).</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you do something every week for several months, then some habits you maintain for e.g. 4 months will not remain at e.g. 5 months. Two examples for me were cleaning (I cleaned right beforehand every week for the first few months, now I don’t really clean for board games specifically) and not eating leftover oreos (I kept oreos as snacks for the first several months, but swapped them out after I started occasionally craving and eating them on other days).</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For snacks, I generally provide pretzels, seltzers, baby carrots, hummus, and (previously) oreos. The pretzels, seltzers, and oreos all keep very well, so I have generally stocked them for a few weeks at a time. I recommend having a stack of disposable paper bowls; they are just very good for snacks. Cost for snacks, etc. has not been an issue for me, but I usually include a little (non-mandatory) ‘Venmo me here if you want to help me keep this going’ note on my event descriptions and have been pleasantly surprised by people doing exactly this.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I initially started hosting, a mistake I made was assuming that dinner was a required feature, so (inspired by </span><a href="https://benorenstein.substack.com/p/a-year-of-sunday-dinners"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ben Orenstein’s Fractal dinners</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) I used Uber Eats’ ‘Group Order’ feature to get everyone’s dinner delivered (it has a nice feature that lets people pay for themselves). The experience of doing this was manageable, but occasionally frustrating, and after discussing with regulars, I dropped it, which simplified things significantly on my end. It is definitely a reasonable option, so I would recommend it if it seems applicable (though I would suggest also trying out DoorDash’s group order option as well).</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another minor mistake I made was buying napkins. Napkins are useless. Everyone knows they are useless. If you have napkins and paper towels, people will always use the paper towels because, unlike napkins, they can turn a ‘mess’ into ‘not a mess’ instead of turning a ‘mess’ into ‘a mess, plus a destroyed napkin.’ This means you will never be rid of your napkins and they will haunt you forever with their uselessness.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anyway, I should mention that I am definitely not the only person in the local community running events (my current events are probably around 35% of the events being run monthly), so a substantial part of my positive experience has included other people hosting interesting events. That said, I think that I can claim credit for creating a space with exactly the kind of consistent social interaction that encourages people to invest in getting to know each other and hosting their own (less regular) events.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, my conclusion from this whole experience is that it is possible to achieve things like “creating community” in sustainable ways through appropriate applications of time, effort, and favorable circumstances. Other than that, I think I’ll just close by saying that the Boston EA/LW/ACX </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conspiracy</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> community is always open to new members, so reach out if you’re in the area and I’ll pass along the community info (discord, </span><a href="https://groups.google.com/g/ssc-boston"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mailing list</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, etc.).</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/y8K3FREw4R7me99ib/reflections-on-37-weeks-of-hosting-ea-lw-acx-board-games#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Go seeking mysteries](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/f5r7FpLWiFQ7aeJu9/go-seeking-mysteries)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think an impactful line of research would be to hunt for unexplained phenomena: simple things that are surprising and interesting, given the most parsimonious explanation of the data. Let’s call this </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mystery-seeking</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 85.1963746223565%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795704611/lexical_client_uploads/msdha4zxmjzq3dxbka3c.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MS Paint drawing by Opus 5.5.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also think this is differentially good for empirics-focused, junior researchers to work on. One of the most important and difficult-to-solve bottlenecks in alignment research is senior researchers who have taste. Mystery-seeking both avoids the problem of requiring deep theoretical agendas, and is conducive in forming a research agenda you can own.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do I mean by this?</span></h3><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The clearest way to draw this out is to give some uncontroversial exemplars of this type of work.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The work of </span><a href="https://truthful.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Truthful AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is probably archetypal here. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">emergent misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, GPT-4o was fine-tuned to write insecure code without telling the user, and it generalised to broader misbehaviour, which was surprising. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14805"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">subliminal learning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a teacher model that likes owls generates sequences of plain numbers, which causes student models (sharing the same base) to also like owls, which is surprising. </span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14345"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Value leakage</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09742"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">weird generalisation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are also examples of this.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23836"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eval awareness paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is another example of this: large language models often know, from subtle cues, that they are being evaluated. This is pretty obvious now, but the mechanisms behind it are quite mysterious.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.10248"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering vectors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> were also surprising, as </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dqSwccGTWyBgxrR58/turntrout-s-shortform-feed?commentId=wKJSBScx4yrMGwSxi"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alex Turner points out.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> At the time it was pretty reasonable to expect that changing high-level behaviour such as sentiment should require training, yet you could do it flexibly and composably with relatively simple activation vectors.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the spiritual bliss attractor (</span><a href="https://assets.claude.com/4263b940cabb546aa0e3283f35b686f4f3b2ff47.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Opus 4 system card</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) is another example, where models tend to converge to loopy text about eternal love and nirvana, after enough self-talk. This is surprising! Why do they end up like this, and not incoherent in some other way?</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QkEyry3Mqo8umbhoK/self-fulfilling-misalignment-data-might-be-poisoning-our-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Self-fulfilling misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is another one, which was easily verified in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10160"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the alignment pretraining paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the presence of discourse about AIs being misaligned makes them behave worse.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also tried to do this type of work, showing that models could be aware when </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12747"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you prefilled off-policy text</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and could be easily trained to be </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21399"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">aware when steered</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I felt I did a reasonable job!</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Systematizing, a good mystery-seeking paper discovers </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">an exciting phenomenon that resists simple explanation.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Let’s unpack this, since that’s not that straightforward! This definition has 3 components: [exciting], [phenomenon], and [resists simple explanation].</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your work must be exciting, which means it’s in a field that captivates the public imagination. It must be relevant to something they care about, or contain technical complexity in a way that’s intrinsically interesting. Obviously, alignment is important, but not all aspects of it are equally important. Some papers deal with the safety of very narrow slices, or deal with problems that are essentially solved or unsolvable, which are unexciting.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One negative example: marginal improvements in tamper robustness and unlearning for open-weight models. Unless there is some huge paradigm changing work here, my intuition is that attacker control is too strong and you’d either need to do large scale filtering or work at the level of regulation to make progress on risks here.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another negative example: trying to measure some grandiose, high-level alignment property (like corrigibility or scheming) but only doing evaluations on small, almost incoherent models.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your work must concern a phenomenon, which means you can coherently describe what you’re measuring, it must be robust, legible, easy to observe. A reader should be able to look at a transcript and say: hey, that’s weird! It must not map on trivially as “this phenomenon, but for something else” because then it wouldn’t be discovering it (but some versions of this are still good, like if that setting is exciting or load-bearing in some way).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your work must resist simple explanations, which means that you must carefully control for the most simple and dumb explanation one can come to given the data. This is to show that you are actually seeing the phenomenon you’re seeing, and that you are justified in calling it unexplained. In papers that get a model to introspect on steering, some simpler explanations one needs to control for are “this steering vector just makes the model pick the first answer”, “the model just answers this for any random vector with sufficient magnitude”, “the model is reading its own steered text and continuing from there”. If you’ve come up with many simple baselines and apply a </span><a href="https://seeingtruly.substack.com/p/sfmaxxing-part-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fine-cuts lens</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but it still exists, you’ve got something exciting!</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One heuristic I use when I want to do this type of paper is to ask a broad sweep of people what they predict the outcome of an experiment to be, or if they think this conclusion would be surprising. You want the answers to be bimodally split between “that’s obvious” and “that’s impossible”, that is the answers being closer to the extremes. You don’t want the answers to be in the middle. This was how we came up with </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03810"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consistency Training Can Entrench Misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: if you encourage some type of consistency in your model (in an unsupervised way), and it’s already misaligned, would it make it more or less misaligned? People seemed split on this, so we went ahead and showed it was a mess. You want a paper that’s at the core of an </span><a href="https://davidafrica.substack.com/p/people-should-resolve-tensions-in"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">interesting tension</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, it’s actually not that simple to come up with these types of papers, as the definition can be quite deep! But that’s fine, if such papers were easy to do, they wouldn’t be worth doing. </span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why should you do this?</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Often, this work is simply useful and has led to useful work: it seems like emergent misalignment helped us understand something about persona-based generalisation in LLMs. Eval awareness was caught somewhat early, before it seemed to confound every behavioural audit (although we weren’t able to avoid worsening it in time, but the advance warning was probably useful). These phenomena can often be central objects of interest to the broader community.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further, if you think about it, the fear around recursive self-improvement (RSI) and sharp left turns rests pretty heavily on unknown unknowns: there will be covert </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/editPost?postId=7wzWuLP3HhyBLmKfo&amp;key=46a14a63d57479687e7a35c3e9eb5a"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">side channels we didn’t think of</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, there will be latent capabilities </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21664"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we didn’t elicit hard enough</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, there will be </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o9CYcjZDqCQf5qeJy/misalignment-traps-lie-in-wait"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">misalignment traps we don’t know of</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. When the automated alignment researchers begin to whirr and iterate at rapid pace, our surface area for unknown unknowns will blow up but our automated evals (especially if LLM fuzzy judgement remains highly correlated) will only catch a narrow slice. One could think of mystery-seeking as a way of stress-testing reality for weird things now, while our fuzzy judgement is still informative.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further, this work is also energizing and inspiring, more likely to snipe people outside to work on this. Clean problem formulations are a good way to get people from other deep backgrounds </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cWFsCFyCttsiJwn2j/bringing-more-expertise-to-bear-on-alignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">with their own expertise</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to apply their deep knowledge! And, it’s fun.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, the main reason I claim we should do this is that curiosity-based research is valuable!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One thing I notice in many AI safety papers is that they are </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incremental-slop</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: taking a thin slice out of something, like unlearning, or jailbreaks, or preventing emergent misalignment, or having a slightly better probe. They’ll make a marginal improvement to the algorithm, or apply a method to a new dataset or language or domain, or show that this phenomenon is not that sound in some way. They stack and compose methods to get a towering and complicated thing, which moves the number up higher. Many of these papers are incremental and unimaginative: they might be good to get into a main conference, but will have ~0 impact in the minds of people or alignment more broadly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem is not that they are engineering-themed. On the contrary, engineering-themed papers are great, exciting, and hard to do. Something as simple as “do probes </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> work in production?” or “ok, but why do LLMs get incoherent in long long contexts?” would be really valuable. The problem is that incremental-slop is unambitious, failing to bring understanding. It lacks curiosity!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you think about how pre-paradigmatic sciences hurtle towards maturity, their scientists did lots of weird things. I thought Gemini 4 Carbon summarized this really well:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before physics had Maxwell’s equations or quantum mechanics—and before biology had molecular genetics—researchers couldn’t just sit in an armchair and deduce grand unified theories, nor did they make progress by optimizing steam engines by 1%. They did natural history and anomaly-hunting: rubbing amber on fur, watching frog legs twitch, noticing weird spectral lines, and isolating phenomena in clean glass tubes until their existing mental models broke. </span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Right now, alignment is thoroughly pre-paradigmatic. But we don’t have the luxury of years of pre-infusion: we must rapidly, aggressively, and furiously collect empirical observation to force theorists to step back and systematize. Good mystery provides stimulus for good theory to arise.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t think everyone should do this. But it seems reasonable to say that junior researchers without a clear theory background would be differentially good here. Why?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It doesn’t require lots of pre-reading, and readily admits agency, human effort.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It builds crucial skills related to taste: looking at the data, doing a good lit review to figure out what would be considered “unexplained” or “weird”, and getting good at </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Ldrss6o3tiKT6NdMm/my-research-process-understanding-and-cultivating-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">predicting the outcomes of experiments</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One can come in without preconceived notions of what’s possible or impossible, which can allow you to </span><a href="https://honorscollege.rutgers.edu/features/undergraduate-upends-40-year-old-data-science-conjecture"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">overturn widely held beliefs with careful observation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How might you go about doing this?</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s one overarching piece of advice: keep it simple, stupid. The more complicated your setup is, the more likely a result comes from a bug in your harness or an artificial choice of dataset. You should think of yourself as having a </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">complexity budget, </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and spend most of that on baselines, ablations, ruling out confounds. Also, the simpler your setup is, the less ablations you have to do!</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s another overarching thing: you want to look at the data. Just, please look at the data. Talk to your model organism. You form a tactile sense of what’s wrong, and avoid deferring to a model explanation, which can miss crucial details. Smushing 5000 rollouts into a line graph can average out all the interesting bits!</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some specific suggestions where to look:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simple things that go wrong with well understood pipelines. Take the standard training pipeline that many people run every day: SFT, DPO, RLVR, MOPD, etc. Do your </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dYnhhTxoDj3fuCxLB/do-your-capabilities-homework"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">capabilities homework</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Then, poke at edge cases where we might be making implicit assumptions. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simple ways that things could go speculatively wrong in the future. There are many theoretical alignment problems that don’t have clear demonstrations or settings yet. Why? Are the models not yet ready? What happens when a model gets a chance in a setting with realistic self-evaluation (unlike this paper, which uses </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23092"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unrealistic self-evaluation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)? Or, take </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/J2kpxLjEyqh6x3oA4/value-systematization-how-values-become-coherent-and"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">value systematization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. What’s the right framework to observe messy, context-dependent heuristics crystallizing into something coherent all of a sudden? Is current reward-seeking behaviour an example of that?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Folklore people take for granted. People say: RLVR only sharpens base-model capabilities, post-training washes out pre-training, prefill is really strong in shaping model default behaviour. Really? When? I’m sure there are many facts we treat as common sense that are actually wrong: a form of alignment zero-day we can aggressively patch.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When shouldn’t you do this? </span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I said, not everyone should work like this. Conditioned on doing safety/alignment research (I don’t have strong takes on this vs. other things that I don’t have experience on, like comms or deep policy or something), there are some cases where you might want to do something else:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You have a clear bet that you think might solve a core piece of alignment. Go execute on that!</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You’re building some important load-bearing piece of infrastructure. Go do disciplined engineering for control harnesses, or build public understanding through embedded evaluation, or something else really impactful!</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You aren’t willing to throw away dead ends. Mystery-seeking is high variance, and will require you to kill your darlings. You have to drop things that aren’t a hell yes, but you might also have to drop a hell yes that isn’t a yes. Rough!</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Still, if you’re early, and empirically minded, and trying to build research taste (as I imagine most are), give this a go for a month or two!</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/f5r7FpLWiFQ7aeJu9/go-seeking-mysteries#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Recursive Self Alignment: The Model is the Estimator](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qvy8Tvhzg2PgfvHcs/recursive-self-alignment-the-model-is-the-estimator)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment research has historically been undertaken from the position that we should solve it before the intelligence explosion. But the intelligence explosion has already begun. We investigate what an alignment paradigm might look like in the time of RSI. This is intended to be the first post in a sequence I’m writing.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model is the estimator</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In classical learning theory, learning decomposes into approximation (e.g. can our model represent the goal?), estimation (how well can we infer it from finite, noisy data?), and optimization (e.g. gradient descent).</span></p><p><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#executive-summary" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR’s October 2026 report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> argues that the source of recent acceleration is not training scale-up, and attributes most of it to AI assistance. </span><a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/pretraining-progress-is-mostly-data" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dwarkesh Patel and Jerry Han argue</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that pretraining progress has mostly come from data quality, while noting that recent gains have come largely from RL. One can imagine the RL analogue being more interesting here: RSIing on data quality might look like having models remove or fix broken or hacky environments.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So we should expect that during RSI, models are not only making all the small decisions about the training process that might impact the incremental alignment of the final model (e.g. forward KL covering support vs reverse KL seeking modes), but also determining their own reward functions (inferring what the researcher actually meant, given the existing, broken signal, and perhaps some messy feedback, in order to rewrite the training signal for the next run). Thus, in a learning theoretic sense, the model itself becomes the estimator in the meta-optimization process that is RSI, with the researcher’s intended reward as the estimand.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this paradigm, the model eventually must become responsible not only for estimating reward, but also for applying alignment techniques like CoT monitoring and interpretability.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefimnuxsis5hg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnimnuxsis5hg">[1]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Incremental alignment wins are often capabilities wins</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prosaic alignment wins found this way lead to capabilities improvements. How? Better environments improve not only specification and estimation (what the RL signal is pointing at) but also optimization (in this case, less noisy and less biased signal). And because the RL signal is better pointed at the objective (solve this coding challenge) rather than a proxy (solve this coding challenge OR reward hack), our process better optimizes for the objective, leading to a model that is both more aligned and more capable, especially per unit time, money, and optimization pressure.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdftcly4vgf9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndftcly4vgf9">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bias in favor of alignment then amounts to favoring generalization over convergence — getting better at hitting the right target over getting better at pumping more optimization pressure into it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is potentially heartening: because incremental improvements to alignment lead to incremental improvements to capabilities, labs have an incentive to build better alignment-estimators and to use them in their RSI loops, which reduces the amount of optimization pressure needed to accomplish any given "prosaic capability". </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8ny1q1tqr7m"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8ny1q1tqr7m">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, stopping a coding model from deleting unit tests is obviously a long way from friendly AI. But as RSI moves into messier, open-ended research tasks, fixing broken rewards starts requiring the model to generalize to actual human intent, and I argue prosaic alignment and deep alignment may sit on the same curve, even if commercial incentives don’t follow that curve by default.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By default, however, we should expect that the capability process will likely converge faster than the alignment process (if the latter converges at all), and we have no guarantees about smoothness. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpkkjlw5yzv8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpkkjlw5yzv8">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It may be important to study ways in which we might be able to empower differential alignment gains over incremental capability gains in this context.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, this “incremental” frame is worth examining. It falls out, somewhat necessarily. of the learning theory framing, but it draws a throughline between prosaic alignment and friendly AI that old-school friendliness researchers will rightly object to. In the next post, I'll discuss why, in the context of modern LLM training, I think there may be a better chance this path exists than we may previously have thought. I’ll lay out a frame of alignment as generalization, capabilities as convergence, and white-box methods like interp (and methods like CoT monitoring) as regularization.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnimnuxsis5hg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefimnuxsis5hg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, I'm not necessarily saying that the model is a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">good</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> estimator by default, just that it is used as one in an RSI loop.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndftcly4vgf9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdftcly4vgf9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More generally, not reward hacking makes the model more useful, because it’s more prosaically aligned to what you want to do, and because cleaner training signals are easier to optimize.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8ny1q1tqr7m"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8ny1q1tqr7m">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> On the other hand, focusing on fewer, less hacky environments doesn't automatically mean less optimization pressure. RL scaling may well be currently bottlenecked on RL environment quality, so fixing them may let labs push optimization even harder.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpkkjlw5yzv8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpkkjlw5yzv8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That is, we don’t know how the shape of (automated) graduate student descent compares to GRPO, and we don’t have a clear picture of how to apply optimization pressure to teach the model to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">act </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on its knowledge of the experimenter.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qvy8Tvhzg2PgfvHcs/recursive-self-alignment-the-model-is-the-estimator#comments">Discuss</a>

### [All I Want (For Christmas) Is P ≠ NP](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dnoewDrL7fAPzw4pb/all-i-want-for-christmas-is-p-np)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>[Intro]<br />
I don't want a lot for Christmas<br />
All those things that you have found<br />
I don't want more algorithms<br />
Don't need no more upper-bounds</p>
<p>[Chorus]<br />
I just want to know there's something<br />
More than you will ever know<br />
It's easy, don't you see?<br />
All I want is "P not NP"!</p>
<p>[Intro]</p>
<p>Navier-Stokes, Yang-Mills, and Riemann<br />
Those are quite enough for me<br />
There's no need to follow up with<br />
Hodge and Birch and Swinny-D</p>
<p>[Chorus]</p>
<p>Goldbach, Collatz, they can wait<br />
Next year, you can take your time<br />
Fast or slow, it makes no difference<br />
Fly and dance to that finish-line</p>
<p>But I don't want some factorizer<br />
Stealing my identity<br />
I don't want a world of hackers<br />
Breaching our security</p>
<p>Oh let me rest for just one night<br />
Tell me that we'll be all right<br />
There's nothing else I need<br />
(Oh baby) All I want is "P not NP"!</p>
<p>Oh, nothing's safe but couriers<br />
And good old one-time pads<br />
Global market turmoil<br />
Things'll get really bad</p>
<p>Remote code execution<br />
Everywhere confusion<br />
Tell me that this nightmare will never come to be<br />
Tell me P doesn't equal NP!</p>
<p>Oh, I don't want a lot for Christmas<br />
This is all that's on my mind<br />
Will there still be some solutions<br />
We can check but never find?</p>
<p>[Chorus]</p>
<p>All I want is "P not NP"! [etc.]</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dnoewDrL7fAPzw4pb/all-i-want-for-christmas-is-p-np#comments">Discuss</a>
